x_{g}x_{h}\in R_{gh}
for all
h\in G
.
Since
\{R_{g}\}_{g}
is a decomposition of
R
we have
x_{1}x_{h}=x_{h}
and thus
x_{1}\cdot 1=1
.
Hence
1=x_{1}\in R_{1}
, and
R_{1}
is a subring of
R
.

As before write
y^{-1}=\sum_{g\in G}z_{g}
with
z_{g}\in R_{g}
for all
g
.
As
R_{1}\ni 1=yy^{-1}=\sum_{g\in G}yz_{g}
it follows that
yz_{h^{-1}}=1
and thus
y^{-1}=z_{h^{-1}}\in R_{h^{-1}}
.
Finally
yR_{1}\subseteq R_{h}=yy^{-1}R_{h}\subseteq yR_{1}
, so that
yR_{1}=R_{h}
.
∎

Lemma 10.3
.

Suppose
R\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
is a subring and
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is a grading of
R
.
Then
H=\{g\in G\,|\,R_{g}\neq 0\}
is a countable torsion subgroup of
G
.
Moreover, if
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is universal, then
G=H
.

Proof.

Since
0
is the only zero-divisor in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have for
g,h\in H
that
0\subsetneq R_{g}R_{h}\subseteq R_{gh}
, so
gh\in H
.
For
g\in H
and
x\in R_{g}
non-zero we have
x^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}x^{i}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
0\neq x^{n}\in R_{g^{n}}\cap\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}R_{g^{i}}
.
Hence
g^{n}=g^{i}
for some
0\leq i<n
, so the order of
g
is finite, and
H
is a torsion group. That it is countable follows from the countability of
R
.

Suppose now that
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is universal.
By what we have shown before
(H,\{R_{h}\}_{h})
is a grading of
R
, and the inclusion
i:H\to G
is a morphism of gradings
(H,\{R_{h}\}_{h})\to(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
of
R
.
By universality there exists a map
u:G\to H
of gradings in the opposite direction.
The composition
i\circ u
is an endomorphism of
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
, which by universality is unique and thus equal to the identity.
Hence
i
is surjective. It follows that
G=H
, as was to be shown.
∎

Definition 10.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
We write
\mu_{n}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
for the group of
n
-th roots of unity.
We equip
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
with a Hilbert lattice structure with the inner product given by

\langle x,y\rangle_{n}=\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}\sum_{\sigma\in\X(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}])}\sigma(x)\cdot\overline{\sigma(y)}

for
x,y\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
and any
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq L\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
[L:\mathbb{Q}]<\infty
and
x,y\in L\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
.

The proof that this is a Hilbert lattice is completely analogous to the proof for
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.
One can show that for
x,y\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
we have
\langle x,y\rangle_{n}=\varphi(n)\langle x,y\rangle
, where
\varphi
is the Euler totient function, using that we have a natural bijection
\X(L)\times\X(\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}])\to\X(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}])
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Suppose
R\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
is a subring and
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is a grading of
R
.
Then for all
g,h\in G
distinct we have
\langle R_{g},R_{h}\rangle=0
.

Proof.

Let
H
be as in Lemma
10.3
.
If
R_{g}=0
or
R_{h}=0
we are done, so suppose
g,h\in H
and let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
g^{n}=1=h^{n}
.
As
\varphi(n)\langle R_{g},R_{h}\rangle=\langle R_{g},R_{h}\rangle_{n}
it suffices to show that
\langle R_{g},R_{h}\rangle_{n}=0
.
Since
g\neq h
there exists by Pontryagin duality some morphism
\chi\in\Hom(H,\mu_{n})
such that
\chi(g)\neq\chi(h)
.
One then verifies that the induced
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
-algebra automorphism
\chi
of
R\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
that maps
x\otimes 1\in R_{i}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
to
x\otimes\chi(i)
for all
i\in H
is an isometry.
Hence for
x\in R_{g}
and
y\in R_{h}
we have

\langle x,y\rangle_{n}=\langle\chi x,\chi y\rangle_{n}=\langle\chi(g)x,\chi(h)y\rangle_{n}=\langle x,\chi(g)^{-1}\chi(h)y\rangle_{n}.

Because
\chi(g^{-1}h)
is a non-trivial
n
-th root of unity,
1-\chi(g^{-1}h)
divides
n
.
Hence

0=\langle R_{g},(1-\chi(g^{-1}h))R_{h}\rangle_{n}\supseteq\langle R_{g},nR_{h}\rangle_{n}=n\langle R_{g},R_{h}\rangle_{n},

and we are done.
∎

Theorem
1.7
.
Every subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
has a universal grading.

Proof.

Let
R
be a subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is also a sublattice of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
\textup{Y}=\{\textup{Y}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a universal decomposition of the lattice
R
, which exists by Theorem
5.4
.
We obtain this decomposition by starting with the graph
\mathcal{G}
on the vertex set
\textup{indec}(R)
with edges between
x,y\in\textup{indec}(R)
if and only if
\langle x,y\rangle\neq 0
, then taking
I
to be the set of connected components of
\mathcal{G}
and
\textup{Y}_{i}
the group generated by
i\in I
.
For
x=\sum_{i}\upsilon_{i}\in R
with
\upsilon_{i}\in\textup{Y}_{i}
write
\textup{supp}(x)=\{i\in I\,|\,\upsilon_{i}\neq 0\}
.
Now consider the free abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{(I)}
and let
U
be the group obtained from it by dividing out

N=\langle i+j-k\,|\,i,j\in I,\,k\in\textup{supp}(\textup{Y}_{i}\cdot\textup{Y}_{j})\rangle.

We have an induced map
f:I\to\mathbb{Z}^{(I)}\to U
which induces a decomposition
f(\textup{Y})
of
R
, which is also a grading.
That it is universal follows from the fact that
Y
is a refinement of
\mathcal{R}
for any grading
(G,\mathcal{R})
by Lemma
10.5
.
∎

Example 10.6
.

From Theorem
1.7
it follows in particular that
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
has a universal grading.
It follows from Lemma
10.5
that such a grading gives an orthogonal decomposition of the lattice.
By Theorem
1.1
such a decomposition is trivial, and thus
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
has a trivial universal grading.

Example 10.7
.

Every countable torsion group occurs as the group of a universal grading of a subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Note that such a group is a subgroup of
\Omega=\bigoplus_{\smash{p\in\mathcal{P}}}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
, where
\mathcal{P}
is some countably infinite set.
We choose
\mathcal{P}
to be the set of positive prime numbers.
Fixing some embedding
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{C}
we have a well-defined
x
-th power of
p
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
[\cdot]:\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}\to[0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
be the (bijective) map that assigns to each class its smallest non-negative representative.
It is then easy to verify that
R=\mathbb{Z}[p^{x}\,|\,p\in\mathcal{P},\,x\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}]\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
has a grading
(\Omega,(R_{(x_{p})_{p}})_{(x_{p})_{p}})
with

R_{(x_{p})_{p}}=\Big(\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}p^{[x_{p}]}\Big)\cdot\mathbb{Z}.

In turn any subgroup
G\subseteq\Omega
gives a grading
(G,(R_{g})_{g})
of the subring
\bigoplus_{g\in G}R_{g}\subseteq R
, and this grading must be universal because a universal grading exists and all
R_{g}
are of rank 1.

Lemma 10.8
.

Suppose
R\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
is a subring and
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is a grading of
R
.
If
K=\mathbb{Q}(A)
for some subset
A\subseteq\bigcup_{g\in G}R_{g}
, then
(G,\{R_{g}\cap K\}_{g})
is a grading of
R\cap K
.

Proof.

It is clear that
(G,\{R_{g}\cap K\}_{g})
is a grading of
R\cap K
once we show
\bigoplus_{g}(R_{g}\cap K)=R\cap K
.
For this it remains to show that
R\cap K\subseteq\sum_{g}(R_{g}\cap K)
.
Let
x\in R\cap K
.
As
x\in R
we may uniquely write
x=\sum_{g}x_{g}
for some
x_{g}\in R_{g}
.
Without loss of generality
A
is closed under multiplication, so that
A
generates
K
as a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space.
Then we may write
x=\sum_{a\in A}r_{a}a
for some
r_{a}\in\mathbb{Q}
which are almost all equal to zero.
Hence a positive integer multiple
\lambda x
of
x
satisfies
\sum_{g}\lambda x_{g}=\lambda x=\sum_{a\in A}\lambda r_{a}a
with
\lambda r_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
a
and thus
\lambda r_{a}a\in R_{g_{a}}
for some
g_{a}\in G
.
It follows from uniqueness of the decomposition that
\lambda x_{g}=\sum_{a\in A,\,g_{a}=g}\lambda r_{a}a
and thus
x_{g}\in K
.
We conclude that
x_{g}\in R_{g}\cap K
and thus
x\in\sum_{g}(R_{g}\cap K)
, as was to be shown.
∎

We may also lift Theorem 1.4 of
[
12
]
to our more general setting.

Theorem
1.8
.
Every integrally closed subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
has a universal grading with a subgroup of
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be an integrally closed subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and let
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
be a universal grading, which exists by Theorem
1.7
.
It suffices to show that every finitely generated subgroup
H
of
G
is cyclic.

Let
H\subseteq G
be finitely generated and thus finite by Lemma
10.3
.
Moreover, by Lemma
10.3
we have
R_{h}\neq 0
for all
h\in H
, so we may choose some non-zero
a_{h}\in R_{h}
.
Let
A=\{a_{h}\mid h\in H\}
and
K=\mathbb{Q}(A)
.
Then by Lemma
10.8
we get a grading
(H,\{R_{h}\cap K\})
of
S=R\cap K
.
Since
K
is a field and
R
is integrally closed, the ring
S
is integrally closed.
As
K
is finite over
\mathbb{Q}
, the same holds for the field of fractions of
S
.
Hence we may apply Theorem 1.4 from
[
12
]
to conclude that the universal grading of
S
has a cyclic grading group
Y
.
By universality we get a morphism of gradings and thus a morphism of groups
Y\to H
.
The latter is surjective since
0\neq R_{h}\cap K\ni a_{h}
for all
h\in H
.
Thus
H
is cyclic, as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma 10.9
.

Suppose
R\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
is a subring and
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is a grading of
R
.
If the universal grading of
R_{1}
is trivial and
R_{g}\neq 0
for all
g\in G
, then
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is universal.

Proof.

Suppose
(H,\{S_{h}\}_{h})
is a universal grading of
R
, which exists by Theorem
1.7
, and let
f:(H,\{S_{h}\}_{h})\to(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
be the map given by universality.
Then
R_{1}=\bigoplus_{h\in\ker(f)}S_{h}
, which is a grading of
R_{1}
.
Since the universal grading of
R_{1}
is trivial, it follows that
R_{1}=S_{1}
.
By Lemma
10.3
we have
S_{h}\neq 0
for all
h\in\ker(f)
, so it follows that
\ker(f)=1
and that
f
is injective.
From the fact that
R_{g}\neq 0
for all
g\in G
it follows that
f
must be surjective.
Thus
f
is an isomorphism of gradings and
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
is universal.
∎

Example 10.10
.

We will show that every subgroup of
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
occurs as the group of a universal grading of an integrally closed subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Recall from Definition
7.12
the notation
\mu_{\infty}
for the group of roots of unity in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For a prime number
p
write
\mu_{p^{\infty}}=\{\zeta\in\mu_{\infty}\,|\,(\exists\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\ \zeta^{p^{n}}=1\}
and
\mu_{p}=\{\zeta\in\mu_{\infty}\,|\,\zeta^{p}=1\}
.
The map
\zeta\mapsto\zeta^{p}
gives an isomorphism
\mu_{p^{\infty}}/\mu_{p}\to\mu_{p^{\infty}}
.
Taking the direct sum over all
p
we get an isomorphism
\mu_{\infty}/\mu_{0}\to\mu_{\infty}
, where
\mu_{0}=\{\zeta\in\mu_{\infty}\,|\,(\exists\,n\text{ square-free})\ \zeta^{n}=1\}
.
Thus it suffices to show that for every
\mu_{0}\subseteq M\subseteq\mu_{\infty}
the group
G=M/\mu_{0}
occurs as a universal grading group.

Consider
R=\mathbb{Z}[M]
, the smallest subring of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
containing
M
, which is integrally closed.
Define
R_{\zeta\cdot\mu_{0}}=\zeta\cdot\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{0}]
for all
\zeta\cdot\mu_{0}\in M/\mu_{0}
and note that this gives a grading
(G,\{R_{g}\}_{g})
of
R
.
To prove this is a universal grading it suffices by Lemma
10.9
to show that the universal grading of
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{0}]
is trivial, or in turn, by Lemma
10.5
, that
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{0}]
is indecomposable.
The elements of
\mu_{0}
are indecomposable in
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{0}]
because they are so in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, and they generate
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{0}]
as an additive group.
From Proposition
8.5
we may conclude that no pair
\zeta,\xi\in\mu_{0}
is orthogonal, so from Theorem
5.4
it follows that
\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{0}]
is indecomposable. Hence the grading is universal.

11.
Enumeration of indecomposable algebraic integers of degree 2

The indecomposables of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
of degree 1 are
1
and
-1
.
In this section we compute the indecomposables of degree 2.
The fields of degree 2 over
\mathbb{Q}
are
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{1\}
square-free.
The following lemma is easily verified by separating the cases
d
negative and positive.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{1\}
be square-free and let
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then
q(a+b\sqrt{d})=a^{2}+|d|\cdot b^{2}
.∎

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and suppose one of the following holds:

(i)

the real part of
\alpha^{2}
is at least
2
under every embedding
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)\to\mathbb{C}
;

(ii)

the real part of
\alpha^{2}
is at most
-2
under every embedding
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)\to\mathbb{C}
;

(iii)

\alpha=(1+\sqrt{d})/2
with
d\in\mathbb{Z}
square-free such that
9\leq d\leq 25
.

Then
\alpha
has a non-trivial decomposition in a degree
2
extension of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and
\gamma=\alpha^{2}/4
.
Let
f=X^{2}-\alpha X+1\in K[X]
, let
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
be a root of
f
and write
L=K(\beta)
.
Then
(\beta-\alpha/2)^{2}=\beta^{2}-\alpha\beta+\alpha^{2}/4=\alpha^{2}/4-1=\gamma-1
.
For (i) and (iii) we will show
q(\beta-\alpha/2)\leq q(\alpha/2)
.
Then
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)
is a decomposition of
\alpha
by Lemma
4.3
, and since neither
0
nor
\alpha
is a root of
f
we conclude that this decomposition is non-trivial.

(i)
Let
\sigma\in\X(L)
.
For
\delta\in L
write
\textup{Re}_{\sigma}(\delta)
and
\textup{Im}_{\sigma}(\delta)
for the real respectively imaginary part of
\sigma(\delta)
.
By assumption
\textup{Re}_{\sigma}(\gamma)\geq 1/2
.
Thus
\textup{Re}_{\sigma}(\gamma-1)^{2}=(\textup{Re}_{\sigma}(\gamma)-1)^{2}\leq\textup{Re}_{\sigma}(\gamma)^{2}
.
As
\textup{Im}_{\sigma}(\gamma-1)^{2}=\textup{Im}_{\sigma}(\gamma)^{2}
we may conclude that
|\sigma(\gamma-1)|\leq|\sigma(\gamma)|
.
Then

\displaystyle q(\beta-\alpha/2)=\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}\sum_{\sigma\in\X(L)}|\sigma(\gamma-1)|\leq\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}\sum_{\sigma\in\X(L)}|\sigma(\gamma)|=q(\alpha/2),

as was to be shown.

(ii)
Let
\textup{i}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
be a primitive fourth root of unity. Then
\textup{i}\alpha
satisfies the conditions to (1), hence it has a non-trivial decomposition
(\beta,\textup{i}\alpha-\beta)
, where
\beta
is a root of
X^{2}-\textup{i}\alpha X+1
.
In turn,
(-\textup{i}\beta,\alpha+\textup{i}\beta)
is a non-trivial decomposition of
\alpha
, where
-\textup{i}\beta
is a root of
X^{2}-\alpha X-1
. In particular
-\textup{i}\beta
is of degree at most
2
over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.

(iii) Since
d>0
the field
K
is totally real.
Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
be the images of
\gamma
under
\X(K)
such that
\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}
. Because
9\leq d\leq 25
we have
\gamma_{1}=(\sqrt{d}-1)^{2}/16\leq 1
and
\gamma_{2}=(\sqrt{d}+1)^{2}/16\geq 1
.
Hence

q(\beta-\alpha/2)=\frac{1}{2}\big(|\gamma_{1}-1|+|\gamma_{2}-1|\big)=\frac{1}{2}\big((1-\gamma_{1})+(\gamma_{2}-1)\big)=\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{16}\leq\frac{1+d}{16}=q(\alpha/2),

as was to be shown.
∎

Theorem 11.3
.

The indecomposable elements of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
of degree
2
up to conjugacy and sign are
\sqrt{-1}
,
\frac{1+\sqrt{-7}}{2}
,
\frac{1+\sqrt{-3}}{2}
and
\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
, for a total of
14
indecomposables.

Proof.

First note that the
4
listed elements indeed are indecomposable:
We treated
(1+\sqrt{-7})/2
in Example
8.3
, and the remaining
3
have square-norm less than
2
, so Proposition
8.1
applies.
Since conjugation and multiplication by
-1
are isometries by Lemma
7.13
, all
14
are indecomposable.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
be of degree
2
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
It remains to show that
\alpha
, up to conjugation and sign, admits a non-trivial decomposition or is one of the
4
listed indecomposables.
Since
\alpha
is of degree 2 over
\mathbb{Q}
it is an element of
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})
for some square-free
d\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{1\}
.
Then we may write
\alpha=(a+b\sqrt{d})/2
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a+b\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and by conjugating and changing sign we may assume
a,b\geq 0
.
If
a\geq 2
we have

q(\alpha/2-1)=\Big(\frac{a}{4}-1\Big)^{2}+|d|\Big(\frac{b}{4}\Big)^{2}=\Big(\Big(\frac{a}{4}\Big)^{2}+|d|\Big(\frac{b}{4}\Big)^{2}\Big)+\Big(1-\frac{a}{2}\Big)\leq q(\alpha/2),

so
(1,\alpha-1)
is a decomposition of
\alpha
.
Since
\alpha\neq 1
, this decomposition is non-trivial.
Similarly we get a decomposition
(\sqrt{d},\alpha-\sqrt{d})
if
b\geq 2
, so either this decomposition is non-trivial or
\alpha=\sqrt{d}
.

First suppose
\alpha=\sqrt{d}
.
If
|d|<2
we have
d=-1
and
\sqrt{-1}
is listed.
Otherwise
\alpha^{2}=d
satisfies the conditions to Lemma
11.2
.i or ii, so
\sqrt{d}
is not indecomposable.
For
\alpha\neq\sqrt{d}
the remaining cases are
\alpha=(1+\sqrt{d})/2
, which is integral only if
d\equiv 1\bmod 4
.
If
d\leq-9
the real part of
\alpha^{2}
is
(1+d)/4\leq-2
under either embedding, so
\alpha
satisfies the conditions to Lemma
11.2
.ii.
If
-9<d<9
we have
d\in\{-7,-3,5\}
and thus
\alpha=(1+\sqrt{d})/2
is listed. For
9\leq d\leq 25
we may apply Lemma
11.2
.iii.
The remaining case is
25<d
, where we have that
\sigma(\alpha^{2})=[(1\pm\sqrt{d})/2]^{2}\geq(\sqrt{d}-1)^{2}/4\geq 2
for all
\sigma\in\X(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d}))
, so Lemma
11.2
.i applies.
Hence
\alpha
is either listed or not indecomposable.
∎

Interesting to note is that all non-trivial decompositions of
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
of degree 2 over
\mathbb{Q}
that are produced in Theorem
11.3
live in a field extension of degree at most 2 over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.

12.
Geometry of numbers

In this section we gather some known results about the geometry of numbers.

Definition 12.1
.

Let
K
be a number field. We write
K_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K
and
K_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}K
.

Recall for a number field
K
the definition of
\X(K)
, the set of ring homomorphisms from
K
to
\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 12.2
.

We have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
\Phi_{K}:K_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\X(K)}
given by

\Phi_{K}(z\otimes\alpha)=(z\cdot\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma\in\X(K)}.

We have a natural inclusion
K_{\mathbb{R}}\to K_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\X(K)}
, and its image is given by the subspace of elements invariant under the involution
(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\mapsto(\overline{x_{\overline{\sigma}}})_{\sigma}
.
This inclusion induces an isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}
-algebras
K_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{s}
for integers
r,s\geq 0
such that
r=\#\{\sigma\in\X(K)\,|\,\sigma[K]\subseteq\mathbb{R}\}
and
r+2s=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
.
∎

Definition 12.3
.

We equip
K_{\mathbb{C}}
with the inner product induced by the standard hermitian inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{\X(K)}
and
K_{\mathbb{R}}
with its restriction, turning
K_{\mathbb{R}}
into a real inner product space. Since
K_{\mathbb{R}}
is an inner product space we have an induced measure on
K_{\mathbb{R}}
we denote
\vol
.

Remark 12.4
.

For a number field
K
and
\alpha\in K
we have
q(\alpha)=\frac{1}{[K:\mathbb{Q}]}\|\Phi_{K}(\alpha)\|^{2}
.
Note that the norm on
K_{\mathbb{R}}
is not the ‘standard’ norm on
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{s}
. In terms of the latter vector space it is given by

(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{r},z_{1},\dotsc,z_{s})\mapsto\sqrt{|x_{1}|^{2}+\dotsm+|x_{r}|^{2}+2|z_{1}|^{2}+\dotsm+2|z_{s}|^{2}}.

Theorem 12.5

(Proposition 4.26 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
R
be an order in a number field
K
. Then
\Phi_{K}[R]
is a full rank lattice in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
with determinant
|\Delta(R)|^{1/2}
, where
\Delta(R)
is the discriminant of
R
. ∎

Definition 12.6
.

For a commutative ring
R
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we write
R[X]_{d}=\{f\in R[X]\,|\,\deg(f)<d\}
.

Lemma 12.7
.

For a number field
K
we have an isomorphism of real vector spaces
K_{\mathbb{R}}[X]_{d}\cong(\mathbb{R}[X]_{d})^{r}\times(\mathbb{C}[X]_{d})^{s}
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
induced by the isomorphism
K_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{s}
of Lemma
12.2
. ∎

Theorem 12.8

(Minkowski, Theorem 4.19 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a full rank lattice and let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a symmetric convex body.
If
\vol(S)>2^{n}\det(\Lambda)
, then there exists a non-zero element in
\Lambda\cap S
. ∎

13.
Szegő capacity theory

In this section we will give a proof of Theorem
1.2
, which is due to T. Chinburg.

Definition 13.1
.

We define for
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and in turn any subfield of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
the
max-norm

|\alpha|_{\infty}=\max_{\sigma\in\X(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}|\sigma(\alpha)|.

One easily shows that the max-norm satisfies the following.

Lemma 13.2
.

For all
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
we have
q(\alpha)\leq|\alpha|_{\infty}^{2}
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
also
|\alpha^{n}|_{\infty}=|\alpha|^{n}_{\infty}
. ∎

In this section we will identify a number field
K
with its image in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We extend
\sigma\in\X(K)
to
K_{\mathbb{C}}
by
z\otimes\alpha\mapsto z\cdot\sigma(\alpha)
or equivalently projecting
\mathbb{C}^{\X(K)}
onto the component at
\sigma
.
We similarly equip
K_{\mathbb{C}}
with a max-norm

\lvert x\rvert_{K}=\max_{\sigma\in\X(K)}|\sigma(x)|.

and
K_{\mathbb{R}}
with the induced norm.
Note that the max-norm
|\,\cdot\,|_{\infty}
of
K
as subfield of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and the max-norm
\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert_{K}
of
K
as subfield of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
agree.

Definition 13.3
.

Let
X
be a metric space with metric
d
and let
S\subseteq X
be a subset.
A
rounding function from
X
to
S
is a map
\lfloor\cdot\rceil:X\to S
for which there exists some constant
c\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that for all
x\in X
we have
d(x,\lfloor x\rceil)\leq c
.
We call such a
c
an
error constant
for
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
.

For
\mathbb{Z}
in
\mathbb{Q}
with the metric induced by the usual absolute value we may round to a nearest integer, giving a rounding function with error constant
1/2
.
For a naive rounding map for an arbitrary order
R
with basis
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
of a number field
K
with metric induced by
q
we may simply send
\sum_{i}x_{i}\alpha_{i}\in K
with
x_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
to
\sum_{i}\lfloor x_{i}\rceil\alpha_{i}\in R
.
An error constant for this rounding function is for example
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\alpha_{i}|_{\infty}
.
However, other rounding functions exist, for example Babai’s nearest plane algorithm, which try to achieve beter error constants.

Lemma 13.4
.

Let
K
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra with a norm
|\cdot|
and let
R
be a subring of
K
.
Let
\alpha\in K
and suppose there is a rounding function
K\to R
with respect to this norm and with error constant
c
.
Consider
K[X]
and let
Y=X-\alpha
.
Then
K[X]=K[Y]
equipped with the max-norm
\|\sum_{i}f_{i}Y^{i}\|\mapsto\max_{i}|f_{i}|
has a rounding function
\lfloor\cdot\rceil:K[X]\to R[X]
with error constant
c
and the following additional property:
If for some
n\geq 0
and
f=\sum_{i}f_{i}X^{i}\in K[X]
we have
f_{k}\in R
for all
k\geq n
, then
f-\lfloor f\rceil
has degree less than
n
.

Proof.

Let
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{1}:K\to R
be the rounding function with error constant
c
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{1}
restricted to
R
is the identity.
With the notation as in Definition
12.6
we identify
K
with
K[X]_{1}
and
R
with
R[X]_{1}
and inductively define for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
a rounding function
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{n}:K[X]_{n}\to R[X]_{n}
.
For
f\in K[Y]_{n+1}
write
f=aY^{n}+g
with
a\in K
and
g\in K[Y]_{n}
.
Now define
\lfloor f\rceil_{n+1}=\lfloor a\rceil_{1}X^{n}+\lfloor g-\lfloor a\rceil_{1}(X^{n}-Y^{n})\rceil_{n}
.
Note that this is well-defined.
Suppose
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{n}
has error constant
c
. Then

\|f-\lfloor f\rceil_{n+1}\|=\max\Big\{\big|a-\lfloor a\rceil_{1}\big|,\,\big\|(g-\lfloor a\rceil_{1}(X^{n}-Y^{n}))-\lfloor g-\lfloor a\rceil_{1}(X^{n}-Y^{n})\rceil_{n}\big\|\Big\}\leq c,

so
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{n+1}
has error constant
c
.
Note that
\lfloor f\rceil_{m}=\lfloor f\rceil_{n}
for
m\geq n
and
f\in K[Y]_{n}
.
Hence this defines a rounding function
K[X]\to R[X]
with error constant
c
as required.
With
f
as before of degree
n
, if
a\in R
then
a-\lfloor a\rceil_{1}=0
and
f-\lfloor f\rceil
has degree less than
n
.
One then inductively proves the final property of
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
.
∎

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
d,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then there exist
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
d\mid\binom{am}{k}
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Proof.

Note that
\binom{X}{k}=X(X-1)\dotsm(X-k+1)/(k!)\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
is a polynomial for fixed
k
. For
X
a multiple of
d\cdot(k!)
we have that
d\mid\binom{X}{k}
.
∎

The following is a special case of a theorem of Szegő, adapted from
[
16
]
.

Theorem 13.6

(Szegő)
.

Let
R
be an order of a number field
K
and let
r>1
.
Then for each
\alpha\in K
there exists a monic non-constant
g\in R[X]
such that for all
z\in K_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}<r
we have
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}<r
.

Proof.

We will write
Y=X-\alpha
and
Y^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}f_{n,i}X^{i}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
d_{0}=1
, and for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
let
d_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be such that
d_{k}\alpha^{k}\in R
.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let
m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be such that for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
d_{k}\mid\binom{am_{k}}{k}
, which exists by Lemma
13.5
.
Then
f_{n,n-k}=\pm\binom{n}{k}\alpha^{k}\in R
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and positive multiples
n
of
m_{k}
and such that
k\leq n
.
Write
M_{b}=\text{lcm}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{b})
.
Then for all
b
and
n
such that
M_{b}\mid n
we have
f_{n,n-k}\in R
for all
0\leq k\leq b
.

Let
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{1}:K\to R
be a rounding function, which exists because
R
is a full rank lattice in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
by Theorem
12.5
.
Let
\lfloor\cdot\rceil:K[Y]\to R[X]
be the rounding function given by Lemma
13.4
and let
c
be its error constant.
For
n\geq 0
let
g_{n}=\lfloor Y^{n}\rceil
and
e_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}e_{n,i}Y^{i}=Y^{n}-g_{n}
.
Moreover, we may assume that if
M_{b}\mid n
for some
0\leq b\leq n
then
\deg(e_{n})<n-b
, as
f_{n,i}\in R
for
i\geq n-b
.
Since
r>1
we may choose
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
2cr^{-b}\leq r-1
.
Next we may choose
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
n=aM_{b}
satisfies
n>b
and
r^{n-1}\geq 2
.
We claim
g=g_{n}
satisfies the requirements.

Clearly
g_{n}
is monic by the bound on the degree of
e_{n}
and non-constant as
n>0
.
Let
\X(K)
act on
K[X]
coefficient-wise and fix
\sigma\in\X(K)
.
Let
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|\sigma(Y)(z)|\geq r
and write
s=|\sigma(Y)(z)|=|z-\sigma(\alpha)|
.
Then

\left|\frac{\sigma(e_{n})(z)}{\sigma(Y^{n})(z)}\right|\leq s^{-n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-b-1}|\sigma(e_{n,i})|\cdot s^{i}\leq s^{-n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-b-1}cs^{i}\leq\frac{cs^{-b}}{s-1}\leq\frac{cr^{-b}}{r-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

It follows that

\left|\frac{\sigma(g_{n})(z)}{\sigma(Y^{n})(z)}\right|=\left|1-\frac{\sigma(e_{n})(z)}{\sigma(Y^{n})(z)}\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\sigma(g_{n})(z)|\geq\frac{|\sigma(Y^{n})(z)|}{2}\geq\frac{r^{n}}{2}\geq r.

Thus, if
|\sigma(g_{n})(z)|<r
, then
|z-\sigma(\alpha)|<r
. Taking the maximum over all
\sigma\in\X(K)
proves the theorem.
∎

Theorem 13.7

(Szegő)
.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>1}
.
Then there exist infinitely many
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
|\alpha-\beta|_{\infty}<r
.

Proof.

Consider
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and let
R\subseteq K
be some order of
K
.
By Theorem
13.6
there exists some monic non-constant
g\in R[X]
such that for all
z\in K_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}<r
we have
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}<r
.
Now
g^{n}-1\in R[X]
is monic and non-constant for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, so
S=\{\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}\,|\,(\exists\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1})\ g(\beta)^{n}=1\}
is infinite.
Let
\beta\in S
and
L=K(\beta)
.
It now suffices to show that
|\alpha-\beta|_{\infty}\leq r^{2}
.

Write
\textup{P}=\prod_{\sigma\in\X(K)}\X_{\sigma}(L)
.
Let
\rho=(\rho_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in\textup{P}
and consider
\beta_{\rho}=(\rho_{\sigma}(\beta))_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}^{\X(K)}=K_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Then

\lvert g(\beta_{\rho})\rvert_{K}^{n}=\lvert g^{n}(\beta_{\rho})\rvert_{K}=\max_{\sigma\in\X(K)}|\rho_{\sigma}(g^{n}(\beta))|=\max_{\sigma\in\X(K)}|\rho_{\sigma}(1)|=1,

so
\lvert g(\beta_{\rho})\rvert_{K}=1<r
.
Thus by definition of
g
we have
\lvert\alpha-\beta_{\rho}\rvert_{K}<r
.
As this holds for all
\rho\in\textup{P}
we have
|\alpha-\beta|_{\infty}=\max_{\rho\in\textup{P}}\,\lvert\alpha-\beta_{\rho}\rvert_{K}<r
, as was to be shown.
∎

Theorem
1.2
.
If
\gamma\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
4<q(\gamma)
, then
\gamma
has infinitely many decompositions in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=\gamma/2
.
By Theorem
13.7
there are infinitely many
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
|\alpha-\beta|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{q(\alpha)}
as
q(\alpha)=q(\gamma)/4>1
.
By Lemma
13.2
each such
\beta
satisfies
q(\alpha-\beta)\leq q(\alpha)
and thus
(\beta,\gamma-\beta)
is a decomposition of
\gamma
by Lemma
4.3
.
∎

It follows from this theorem, as we will show later in the form of Proposition
15.4
, that there are only finitely many indecomposables in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
of a given degree.
Recall the definition of
r
from Definition
6.1
.

Proposition 13.8
.

The Hilbert lattice
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
has a covering radius
2^{-1/4}\leq r(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})\leq 1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.6
we have
2^{3/2}\leq\sup\{q(\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in\textup{indec}(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})\}
and consequently we get the lower bound
2^{-1/4}\leq\sup\{\|\alpha/2\|\,|\,\alpha\in\textup{indec}(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})\}
.
For any
\alpha\in\textup{indec}(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})
we have
\|\alpha/2\|\leq\|\alpha/2-x\|
for all
x\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
by Lemma
4.3
, and thus
\alpha/2\in\overline{\vor}(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})
by Corollary
6.12
.
Therefore
2^{-1/4}\leq r(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})
by Proposition
6.10
.
For all
r>1
and
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
there exist
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\|\alpha-\beta\|\leq r
by Theorem
13.7
.
Taking the limit of
r
down to
1
and noting that
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathbb{Q}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
is dense in the Hilbert space of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
proves the proposition.
∎

14.
Computing decompositions of algebraic integers of large square-norm

The proof of Theorem
13.7
and in turn Theorem
1.2
is constructive and rather explicit as well.
It is not too difficult to design an algorithm that given
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, an order
R
and an
\alpha\in R
such that
q(\alpha)>4
, computes
n
distinct non-trivial decompositions of
\alpha
.
However, we will have to formalize how we encode the input and output.
Moreover, since
q
maps to
\mathbb{R}
we also have to deal with non-exact arithmetic.

Convention 14.1
.

We presuppose that we have decided on a way of encoding
integers

n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
pairs

(a,b)
such that the length
\textup{len}(n)
of the encoding of
n
grows at a rate
O(\log|n|)
and
\textup{len}((a,b))=O(\textup{len}(a)+\textup{len}(b))
. We then encode a
rational
as a pair of integers, a numerator and denominator.
We encode a finite sequence of integers, a
list
, as nested pairs.
An order is a commutative ring which is free and finitely generated as
\mathbb{Z}
-module.
We encode an
order
as a non-negative integer
n
, its rank, together with integers
(a_{ijk})_{1\leq i,j,k\leq n}
such that
e_{i}\cdot e_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{ijk}e_{k}
, where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
We represent a
number field

K
with an order
R\subseteq K
such that
\mathbb{Q}R=K
.
A
homomorphism of modules
out of a free
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
-module is encoded by a list of images of the basis elements.
Given an encoding for a commutative ring
R
we encode a
polynomial
in
R[X]
as an integer
d
, its degree (with
\deg(0)=-1
), and a list of its
d+1
coefficients, i.e. a dense encoding.

Theorem 14.2
.

There exist polynomial-time algorithms for the following problems: Take as input a number field
K
and

(i)

an irreducible polynomial
f\in K[X]
, and compute the number field
K[X]/(f)
and the natural inclusion
K\to K[X]/(f)
;

(ii)

an element
\alpha\in K
, and compute the minimal polynomial of
\alpha
over
\mathbb{Q}
;

(iii)

a non-zero polynomial
f\in K[X]
, and compute the monic irreducible factors of
f
over
K
.

There also exists an algorithm that takes as input a number field
K
and

(iv)

a polynomial
f\in K[X]
, and computes a splitting field of
f
over
K
.

Proof.

Part (i) and (ii) are basic linear algebra. Part (iii) is a result due to A. K. Lenstra
[
11
]
. Part (iv) is a repeated application of (iii) and (i).
∎

Definition 14.3
.

An
approximation
of a real number
x\in\mathbb{R}
is an algorithm that takes as input a positive integer
n
written in unary and computes a rational number
x(n)
such that
|x-x(n)|\leq 2^{-n}
.
We define the analogous notion for complex numbers as well by considering the real and imaginary parts separately.
A number for which there exists an approximation is called
computable
.
An
algebraic real number

x
is encoded as a minimal polynomial
f
over
\mathbb{Q}
of
x
together with an approximation of
x
.

Since there are only countably many algorithms and uncountably many real numbers, not every real number is computable.

Lemma 14.4

(For example
[
1
]
)
.

There is an algorithm that takes as input a non-zero polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
and computes approximations for all complex roots of
f
with multiplicities. ∎

As a direct consequence of Lemma
14.4
and Theorem
14.2
.ii we have the following proposition.

Proposition 14.5
.

There is an algorithm that takes as input an algebraic number
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
represented by its minimal polynomial and computes an approximation of
q(\alpha)
,
N(\alpha)
and
|\alpha|_{\infty}
. ∎

Proposition 14.6
.

There is an algorithm that takes as input an algebraic real number
x
and algebraic numbers
\alpha,\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and decides whether
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=x
.

Proof.

Compute the normal closure
K
of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,\beta)
using Theorem
14.2
.iv and compute the set
A
of conjugates of
\alpha
in
K
using Theorem
14.2
.iii and similarly for
\beta
.
Choose an embedding
\rho\in\X(K)
.
For all
\sigma\in\X(K)
there exist
\alpha_{\sigma}\in\textup{A}
and
\beta_{\sigma}\in\textup{B}
such that
\sigma(\alpha)=\rho(\alpha_{\sigma})
and
\overline{\sigma(\beta)}=\rho(\beta_{\sigma})
because
K
is Galois, and we may actually compute them since there are only finitely many candidates.
Factor the minimal polynomial
f
of
x
over
K
using Theorem
14.2
.
Find a
y
among the roots of
f
in
K
such that
\rho(y)=x
.
If it does not exist, then
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\neq x
.
Otherwise we may decide
\tfrac{1}{[K:\mathbb{Q}]}\sum_{\sigma\in\X(K)}\alpha_{\sigma}\beta_{\sigma}=y
using exact arithmetic in
K
.
∎

Corollary 14.7
.

There is an algorithm that takes as input algebraic integers
\alpha
, given by a minimal polynomial over
\mathbb{Q}
, and
\beta
, given by a minimal polynomial over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
, and decides whether
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
14.6
we can decide whether
\langle\beta,\alpha-\beta\rangle=0
.
If not, then after finitely many steps approximating
\langle\beta,\alpha-\beta\rangle
using Proposition
14.5
we may decide whether
\langle\beta,\alpha-\beta\rangle\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 14.8
.

For every order
R
in a number field
K
and
\alpha\in K
there exists a rounding function
\lfloor\cdot\rceil:K[X-\alpha]\to R[X]
as in Lemma
13.4
, which is computable and has a computable error constant.

Proof.

Note that the naive rounding function
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{1}:K\to R
with respect to a basis
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}
of
R
is computable and has error constant
\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\alpha_{i}|_{\infty}
, which is computable by Proposition
14.5
. Finally note that the error function from Lemma
13.4
is trivially computable given that
\lfloor\cdot\rceil_{1}
is computable, and it has the same computable error constant.
∎

Theorem 14.9
.

There is an algorithm that takes as input an order
R
in a number field
K
, a rational number
r>1
and an
\alpha\in K
, and computes a monic non-constant polynomial
g\in R[X]
such that for all
z\in K_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}<r
we have
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}<r
.

Proof.

Let
\lfloor\cdot\rceil:K[X-\alpha]\to R[X]
be the rounding map from Lemma
14.8
.
Then

(1)

Compute some rational error constant
c
for
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
;

(2)

Compute some
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
d\alpha\in R
by clearing denominators;

(3)

Compute some
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
2cr^{-b}\leq r-1
;

(4)

Compute an
n\in\mathbb{Z}\cdot d^{b}\cdot b!
such that
n>b
and
r^{n-1}\geq 2
;

(5)

Compute and return
\lfloor(X-\alpha)^{n}\rceil
.

The algorithm is correct by the proof of Theorem
13.6
.
∎

Theorem 14.10
.

There is an algorithm that takes as input an
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and element
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
given by its minimal polynomial, and decides whether
q(\alpha)>4
and if so computes
n
non-trivial
(\beta,\gamma)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
each represented by the minimal polynomial of
\beta
over
\mathbb{Z}[\alpha]
.

Proof.

To decide
q(\alpha)>4
, first decide whether
q(\alpha)=4
using Proposition
14.6
. If not, approximating
q(\alpha)
will yield either
q(\alpha)<4
or
q(\alpha)>4
after finitely many steps.
We then follow the proof of Theorem
13.7
and Theorem
1.2
.

Since
q(\alpha)>4
by assumption we may compute using Proposition
14.5
a rational
r
such that
1<r\leq\sqrt{q(\alpha/2)}
.
Compute using Theorem
14.2
.i the number field
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and its order
R=\mathbb{Z}[\alpha]
.
Compute using Theorem
14.9
a monic non-constant
g\in R[X]
such that for all
z\in K_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}<r
we have
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}<r
. Compute the monic irreducible factors of
g^{k}-1
over
K
using Theorem
14.2
.iii for
k
ranging from
1
to infinity until you have found among all of them
n
distinct integral polynomials that are not equal to
X
or
X-\alpha
.
By the proof of Theorem
13.7
this process terminates and produces non-trivial decompositions.
∎

15.
Bounds on indecomposable algebraic integers

In this section we will prove an effective upper bound on the total number of indecomposable algebraic integers of a given degree.
In particular, we will show that this number is finite.
We do this by constructing a complete list of candidates for indecomposability among all algebraic integers of given degree.
We alo give a lower bound on the number of indecomposables.

Proposition 15.1
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\textup{indec}(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})
has minimal polynomial
f=\sum_{k=0}^{n}f_{n-k}X^{k}\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
of degree
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Then
|f_{k}|\leq\binom{n}{k}2^{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq n
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the roots of
f
. We have Maclaurin’s inequalities (Theorem 11.2 in
[
3
]
)

s_{1}\geq s_{2}^{1/2}\geq s_{3}^{1/3}\geq\dotsm\geq s_{n}^{1/n},\quad\text{where }s_{k}=\binom{n}{k}^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\{1,\dotsc,n\}\\
|I|=k\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in I}|\alpha_{i}|.

By Theorem
1.2
we have that
q(\alpha)\leq 4
.
Then by Lemma
2.14
we have

s_{1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}|\alpha_{i}|\leq\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}=\sqrt{q(\alpha)}\leq 2.

Then
|f_{k}|\leq\binom{n}{k}s_{k}\leq\binom{n}{k}s_{1}^{k}\leq\binom{n}{k}2^{k}
for all
k
, as was to be shown.
∎

Corollary 15.2
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\textup{indec}(\overline{\mathbb{Z}})
has degree at most
m
. Then there exists a monic polynomial
g=\sum_{k=0}^{m}g_{m-k}X^{k}\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
of degree
m
such that
g(\alpha)=0
and
|g_{k}|\leq\binom{m}{k}2^{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq m
.

Proof.

Let
f
as in Proposition
15.1
and
g=X^{m-n}\cdot f
. Then
|g_{k}|=|f_{k}|\leq\binom{n}{k}2^{k}\leq\binom{m}{k}2^{k}
.
∎

Proposition 15.3
.

Considered as functions of
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the following hold:

(i)

\displaystyle\log(n!)

\displaystyle=n\log n-n+O(\log n);

(ii)

\displaystyle\log\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{n}k^{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\log n-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}+O(n\log n);

(iii)

\displaystyle\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}(k!)\Big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\log n-\tfrac{3}{4}n^{2}+O(n\log n);

(iv)

\displaystyle\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}+O(n\log n).

Proof.

(i) This is Stirling’s approximation, which is classical.

(ii) Note that
f(x)=x\log x
is an increasing function on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
. Hence

\log\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{n}k^{k}\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}f(k)\leq\int_{1}^{n+1}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}x^{2}\log(x)-\tfrac{1}{4}x^{2}\Big]_{x=1}^{n+1}=\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\log(n)-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}+O(n\log(n)).

We analogously get the same estimate for a lower bound by considering
\int_{1}^{n}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

(iii) From (i) and (ii) we get

\displaystyle\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}(k!)\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big(k\log(k)-k+O(\log(k))\Big)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\log(n)-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}+O(n\log(n)).

(iv) We first rewrite the binomials in terms of factorials and then apply (i) and (iii), so that

\displaystyle\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\log\bigg(\frac{(n!)^{n}}{\big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}(k!)\big)^{2}}\bigg)=n\log(n!)-2\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}(k!)\Big)

\displaystyle=(n^{2}\log(n)-n^{2})-2(\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\log n-\tfrac{3}{4}n^{2})+O(n\log n)=\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}+O(n\log n),

as was to be shown.
∎

Proposition 15.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. There are at most

n\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Big(2\binom{n}{k}2^{k}+1\Big)=\exp\Big(\tfrac{\log(2)+1}{2}\,n^{2}+O(n\log(n))\Big)

indecomposable elements in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
of degree up to
n
.

Proof.

By Corollary
15.2
every indecomposable of degree at most
n
is the root of a monic polynomial
f=\sum_{k=0}^{n}f_{n-k}X^{k}
such that
|f_{k}|\leq\binom{n}{k}2^{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq n
. Hence every such polynomial corresponds to at most
n
indecomposables.
For every
0<k\leq n
there are
2\binom{n}{k}2^{k}+1
choices for
f_{k}
, and
f_{0}=1
, proving the first upper bound.
We may bound
2\binom{n}{k}2^{k}+1\leq 3\binom{n}{k}2^{k}
, so that by Proposition
15.3
.iv we get

n\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Big(2\binom{n}{k}2^{k}+1\Big)\leq n\cdot 3^{n}\cdot 2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\cdot\prod_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}=\exp\Big(\tfrac{\log(2)+1}{2}\,n^{2}+O(n\log(n))\Big),

as was to be shown.
∎

For
f\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
monic write
q(f)
for the average of the square length of the roots of
f
in
\mathbb{C}
, such that for all
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
with minimal polynomial
f_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
we get
q(\alpha)=q(f_{\alpha})
.
Note that
f=(X+2)^{n}
, although it is not irreducible, has
q(f)=4
and attains the bounds of Proposition
15.1
.
However, that does not imply that Proposition
15.4
cannot be improved, as it is not clear that all combinations of coefficients occur for polynomials
f
with
q(f)\leq 4
.
Some small degree numerical results might suggest improvements can be made.

degree

1

2

3

4

# monic
f\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
satisfying the conclusion to Proposition
15.1

5

81

5525

1786785

# monic
f\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
satisfying
q(f)\leq 4

5

49

989

48422

#
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying
q(\alpha)\leq 4

5

39

739

40354

We also have the following lower bound.

Proposition 15.5
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. There are at least

\exp\Big(\frac{\log 2}{4}n^{2}+O(n\log n)\Big)

indecomposable algebraic integers of degree
n
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and recall the definition of
\mathbb{Z}[X]_{n}
from Definition
12.6
. Consider the set

S_{n}=\left\{f=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}f_{k}X^{k}\in 2X\mathbb{Z}[X]_{n-1}+2\,\middle|\,(\forall k)\ |f_{k}|(\sqrt{2})^{k}n\leq(\sqrt{2})^{n-1}\right\}.

For
f\in S_{n}
consider
g=X^{n}-f
and note that
g
is irreducible by Eisenstein’s criterion.
Consider the ball
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
of radius
r=(\sqrt{2})^{1-1/n}<\sqrt{2}
around
0
.
For all
z
on the boundary of
D
we have

|f(z)|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|f_{k}||z|^{k}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|f_{k}|(\sqrt{2})^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{(i)}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(\sqrt{2})^{n-1}}{n}=(\sqrt{2})^{n-1}=|z|^{n},

where (i) is strict for
n
sufficiently large due to
|f_{0}|n=2n<(\sqrt{2})^{n-1}
.
Hence by Rouché’s Theorem the polynomials
X^{n}
and
g
have the same number of roots in
D
.
It follows that all roots of
g
in
\mathbb{C}
have length less than
\sqrt{2}
.
Thus
q(\alpha)<2
for all roots
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
of
g
, so
\alpha
is indecomposable by Proposition
8.1
.

We conclude that for
n
sufficiently large there are at least
n\cdot\#S_{n}
indecomposable algebraic integers of degree
n
, so it remains to prove a lower bound on
\#S_{n}
.
Note that the coefficients of
f\in S_{n}
satisfy independent inequalities, so we may simply give a lower bound per coefficient.
Let
B=n-3\log_{2}(n)-2
, which is positive for
n
sufficiently large.
For
k>B
we consider only
f_{k}=0
and get a lower bound of
1
for this coefficient.
For
0<k\leq B
we have

2\left\lfloor\frac{(\sqrt{2})^{n-k-1}}{2n}\right\rfloor+1\geq 2\Big(\frac{(\sqrt{2})^{n-k-1}}{2n}-1\Big)+1=\frac{(\sqrt{2})^{n-k-1}}{n}-1=\textup{(ii)}

choices for
f_{k}
.
Then for
n
sufficiently large we have

\frac{n}{(\sqrt{2})^{n-k-2}}\leq\frac{n}{n^{3/2}}\leq\sqrt{2}-1,\quad\text{so that}\quad\textup{(ii)}\geq\frac{(\sqrt{2})^{n-k-2}}{n}.

Hence
S_{n}
contains, for
n
sufficiently large, at least

\prod_{k=1}^{B}\frac{(\sqrt{2})^{n-k-2}}{n}=\exp\Big(\frac{\log 2}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{B}(n-k-2)-B\log n\Big)=\exp\Big(\frac{\log 2}{4}n^{2}+O(n\log n)\Big)

elements, from which the proposition follows.
∎

Corollary 15.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. There are at least

\exp\Big(\frac{\log 2}{4}n^{2}+O(n\log n)\Big)

indecomposable algebraic integers of degree up to
n
. ∎

From the upper and lower bound we may now conclude the following.

Theorem
1.3
.
There are least
\exp(\frac{1}{4}(\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n))
and at most
\exp(\frac{1}{2}(1+\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n))
indecomposable algebraic integers of degree up to
n
. ∎

16.
Fekete capacity theory

In this section we present a proof of a special case of Fekete’s theorem using Minkowski’s convex body theorem.
Fekete’s theorem can be thought of as a partial converse to Theorem
13.7
of Szegő.
Although this does not give us a converse to Theorem
1.2
, using similar techniques as in this section we will later prove Theorem
1.4
in Section
19
.
The goal of this section is to showcase the proof technique we will use to prove Theorem
1.4
so that we may later improve clarity by brevity.
Recall for
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
the definition of the norm
|\alpha|_{\infty}=\max_{\sigma\in\X(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}|\sigma(\alpha)|
from Section
13
.

Theorem 16.1

(Fekete)
.

For each
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and
0<r<1
there exist only finitely many
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
|\beta-\alpha|_{\infty}\leq r
.

Just like for Szegő’s theorem, it is possible to derive an algorithmic counterpart to Fekete’s theorem.

Combining Theorem
16.1
and Theorem
13.7
, the point
r=1
is still a singularity.
For
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and
r=1
clearly all
\beta\in\alpha+\mu_{\infty}
satisfy
|\beta-\alpha|_{\infty}\leq r
.
However, when
\alpha\not\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
we do not know what happens in general.
We start with a volume computation.

Definition 16.2
.

Let
A
be an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra equipped with a real inner product.
We equip
A[Y]
with an inner product

\Big\langle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}Y^{k},\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}g_{k}Y^{k}\Big\rangle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\langle f_{k},g_{k}\rangle,

which is the ‘standard’ inner product when we naturally identify
A[Y]
with
A^{(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we equip
A[Y]_{n}
, as defined in Definition
12.6
, with the restriction of this inner product.

Remark 16.3
.

Obviously
\mathbb{R}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with a real inner product.
We identify
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by choosing
\mathbb{R}
-basis
\{1,\textup{i}\}
, and equip
\mathbb{C}
with the inner product induced by the natural inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Note that this inner product in turn induces the standard norm of
\mathbb{C}
.
For a number field
K
we remark that
K_{\mathbb{R}}
has a real inner product as in Definition
12.3
.

Lemma 16.4
.

Let
A
be an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of dimension
d<\infty
.
For all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
c\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have an
\mathbb{R}
-linear transformation
\phi
on
A[X]_{n}
given by
f\mapsto bf(a(X-c))
with
\det\phi=(a^{n(n-1)/2}\cdot b^{n})^{d}
.

Proof.

Note that
\phi
is trivially an
\mathbb{R}
-linear transformation.
Choose an
\mathbb{R}
-basis
\{e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}\}
for
A
.
Writing
\phi
as a matrix with respect to the basis
\{e_{i}X^{j}\,|\,1\leq i\leq d,\,0\leq j<n\}
for
A[X]_{n}
we note that
\phi
is a lower triangular matrix with diagonal entries
b,ba,ba^{2},\dotsc,ba^{n-1}
, each occurring with multiplicity
d
. The determinant of
\phi
is then simply the product of the diagonal.
∎

Lemma 16.5
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
and let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
consider

S_{n}(r)=\{f\in\mathbb{F}[Y]_{n}\,|\,(\forall\,z\in\mathbb{C})\ |z|\leq r\Rightarrow|f(z)|\leq r\}.

Then as function of
n
we have
\log\vol(S_{n}(r))\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\cdot[\mathbb{F}:\mathbb{R}]\cdot\log r+O(n\log n)
.

Proof.

Write
S_{n}=S_{n}(1)
.
By applying the transformation
f\mapsto rf(r^{-1}Y)
to
\mathbb{F}[Y]_{n}
we bijectively map
S_{n}
to
S_{n}(r)
.
From Lemma
16.4
it follows that
\log\vol(S_{n}(r))=-\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}\cdot[\mathbb{F}:\mathbb{R}]\log(r)+\log\vol(S_{n})+O(n\log n)
.
It remains to prove
\log\vol S_{n}\geq O(n\log n)
.

First suppose
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
.
Consider the set

T_{n}=\Big\{\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}f_{k}Y^{k}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n}\,\Big|\,\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|f_{k}|\leq 1\Big\}.

Note that for all
f\in T_{n}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|z|\leq 1
we have
|f(z)|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|f_{k}|\leq 1
, so
f\in S_{n}
.
Hence
T_{n}\subseteq S_{n}
and
\vol(T_{n})\leq\vol(S_{n})
.
With Proposition
15.3
we compute
\log\vol(T_{n})=\log(2^{n}/n!)=O(n\log n)
, from which the lemma follows for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
.

For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, note that we have an isometry
\mathbb{R}[X]_{n}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}[X]_{n}
given by
(f,g)\mapsto f+\textup{i}\cdot g
.
For
f,g\in\tfrac{1}{2}T_{n}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|z|\leq 1
we have
|f(z)+\textup{i}\cdot g(z)|\leq|f(z)|+|g(z)|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}=1
, so
f+\textup{i}\cdot g\in S_{n}
.
Hence
\log\vol(S_{n})\geq\log(\vol(\tfrac{1}{2}T_{n})^{2})=2\log\vol(T_{n})-2n\log 2=O(n\log n)
, from which the lemma follows for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
.
∎

Theorem 16.6
.

Let
R
be an order of a number field
K
and let
0<r<1
.
For all
\alpha\in K
there exists a non-zero
g\in R[X]
such that for all
z\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
, if
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}\leq r
then
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}\leq r
.

Proof.

Write
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and consider the lattice
\Lambda_{n}=R[X]_{n}
in the inner product space
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}
, where
Y=X-\alpha
.
Note that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}=dn
and that
\Lambda_{n}
is a full-rank lattice in
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}
with
\det(\Lambda_{n})=|\Delta(R)|^{n/2}
by Lemma
16.4
and Theorem
12.5
.
Consider

S_{n}=\big\{f\in K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}\,\big|\,(\forall\,\sigma\in\X(K))\,(\forall\,z\in\mathbb{C})\ |z|\leq r\Rightarrow|\sigma(f)(z)|\leq r\big\}

and note that it is both symmetric and convex.
Moreover, it follows from Lemma
16.5
that
\log\vol(S_{n})\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}d\log r+O(n\log n)
. Hence

\log\Big(\frac{\vol(S_{n})}{2^{dn}\cdot\det(\Lambda_{n})}\Big)\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}n^{2}d\log r+O(n\log n).

Because
-\tfrac{1}{2}d\log r>0
there exists some
n
sufficiently large such that
\vol(S_{n})>2^{dn}\det(\Lambda_{n})
.
By Theorem
12.8
there then exists some non-zero
g\in\Lambda_{n}\cap S_{n}
which as polynomial in
X
satisfies the requirements.
∎

Proof of Theorem
16.1
.

Let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and let
R\subseteq K
be some order of
K
.
Then by Theorem
16.6
there exists some non-zero
g\in R[X]
such that for all
z\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
, if
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}\leq r
then
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}\leq r
.
Suppose
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
|\beta-\alpha|_{\infty}\leq r
and let
L=K(\beta)
.
Let
(\rho_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in\textup{P}=\prod_{\sigma\in\X(K)}\X_{\sigma}(L)
such that
\overline{\rho_{\sigma}}=\rho_{\overline{\sigma}}
for all
\sigma\in\X(K)
, and consider
z=(\rho_{\sigma}(\beta))_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\lvert z-\alpha\rvert_{K}\leq|\beta-\alpha|_{\infty}\leq r
, so
\lvert g(z)\rvert_{K}\leq r
.
Consequently we have
|\rho(g(\beta))|\leq r
for all
\rho\in\X(L)
.
Hence

|N_{L/\mathbb{Q}}(g(\beta))|=\prod_{\rho\in\X(L)}|\rho(g(\beta))|\leq r^{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}<1.

As
g(\beta)\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, we must then have
g(\beta)=0
.
As
\beta
must be a root of
g
and
g
is non-zero, there can only be finitely many
\beta
.
∎

17.
Reduction to exponentially bounded polynomials

We now prepare to prove Theorem
1.4
.
If there are only finitely many decompositions of an algebraic integer
\alpha
, then certainly there exists a non-zero polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
such that
f(\beta)=0
for all decompositions
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)
of
\alpha
.
The goal is to exhibit such a polynomial when
q(\alpha)
is small using a lattice argument, similarly to the proof of Theorem
16.1
.
In this section we derive an analytic sufficient condition for a polynomial
f
to have this property.

Definition 17.1
.

Let
K
be a number field.
We define
\mathcal{S}(K)=\X(K)\times\mathbb{C}
, the coproduct (i.e. disjoint union) of measurable spaces of
\#\X(K)
copies of
\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathbb{C}
has the standard Lebesgue measurable space structure.
We write
\mathcal{M}(K)
for the set of probability measures
\mu
on
\mathcal{S}(K)
, i.e. all
\mu
such that
\mu(\mathcal{S}(K))=1
.

Definition 17.2
.

Let
K
be a number field and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
For
f\in K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]
we say
f
is
exponentially bounded at radius
r
if for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K)
satisfying
\int|z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<r^{2}
it holds that
\int\log|\sigma(f)(z)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<0
.

Proposition 17.3
.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
,
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and
r>\sqrt{q(\alpha/2)}
.
If
f\in\mathcal{O}_{K}[X]
is exponentially bounded at radius
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as polynomial in the variable
Y=X-\alpha/2
, then for all
(\beta,\gamma)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
we have
f(\beta)=0
.

Proof.

Suppose
(\beta,\gamma)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
.
Then
q(\beta-\alpha/2)\leq q(\alpha/2)<r^{2}
by Lemma
4.3
.
Let
L=K(\beta)
and

B=\{(\sigma,\rho(\beta-\alpha/2))\in\mathcal{S}(K)\,|\,\rho\in\X_{\sigma}(L)\},

which has
\#B=[L:\mathbb{Q}]
and
\#(B\cap(\{\sigma\}\times\mathbb{C}))=[L:K]
for all
\sigma\in\X(K)
.
Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K)
be the uniform probability measure on
B
and write
f_{Y}
for
f
as a polynomial in the variable
Y
.
Because
\int|x|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,x)=q(\beta-\alpha/2)<r^{2}
and
f_{Y}
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
we get

\displaystyle\log\big(N(f(\beta))^{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}\big)

\displaystyle=\log\prod_{\rho\in\X(L)}|\rho(f(\beta))|=\sum_{\rho\in\X(L)}\log|\rho(f_{Y}(\beta-\alpha/2))|

\displaystyle=[L:\mathbb{Q}]\cdot\int\log|\sigma(f_{Y})(x)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,x)<0.

We conclude that
N(f(\beta))<1
. Since
f(\beta)
is integral we have
f(\beta)=0
, as was to be shown.
∎

Example 17.4
.

The set of polynomials of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
exponentially bounded at radius
r
is closed under multiplication and symmetric.
However, we will show that it is not convex.

Let
r=1
and
K=\mathbb{Q}
.
For all
c\in(-1,1)
the constant polynomial
c
is trivially exponentially bounded at any positive radius, in particular at radius
1
.
Also the polynomial
Y^{2}
is exponentially bounded:
For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K)
such that
\int|z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<1
we have

\int\log|z^{2}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)\leq\log\int|z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<\log 1=0.

Here the first inequality is Jensen’s inequality for integrals. When
\mu
has finite support, this comes down to Lemma
7.5
.
For
c\in(-1,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the product
cY^{2k}
of exponentially bounded polynomials at radius
1
is exponentially bounded at radius
1
.
We claim that
\tfrac{1}{4}(1+Y^{2k})
for
k
sufficiently large, which is a convex combination of
\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\tfrac{1}{2}Y^{2k}
, is not exponentially bounded at radius
1
.
Taking
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Q})
with weight
\tfrac{1}{5}
at
2
and remaining weight at
0
we have
\int|z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)=\tfrac{4}{5}<1
, yet
\int\log|\tfrac{1}{4}(1+Y^{2k})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)=\tfrac{1}{5}\log(1+2^{2k})-\log 4\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
.
We conclude that the set of exponentially bounded polynomials at radius
1
is not convex.
A similar argument works for all radii and number fields.

Lemma 17.5
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be a convex subset and let
f:D\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic.
Then for distinct
x,y\in D
we have

\bigg|\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}\bigg|\leq\sup_{z\in D}|f^{\prime}(z)|.

Proof.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to D
be the parametrization of the straight line connecting
x
and
y
, which is well-defined since
D
is convex. First note that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(\gamma(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(tx+(1-t)y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{x-y}\Big[f(tx+(1-t)y)\Big]_{t=0}^{1}=\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}.

Then

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(\gamma(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\bigg|\leq\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{\prime}(\gamma(t))\big|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{1}\big(\sup_{z\in D}|f^{\prime}(z)|\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\sup_{z\in D}|f^{\prime}(z)|,

as was to be shown.
∎

We will now translate the measure theoretic property of Definition
17.2
to an analytic one.
Our results in the coming sections only depend on the ‘only if’ part of the following equivalence.

Theorem 17.6
.

Let
K
be a number field,
0<r<1
and
f\in K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]
.
Then
f
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
if and only if there exist
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
\sigma\in\X(K)
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have
|\sigma(f)(z)|\leq\exp(a(|z|^{2}-r^{2}))
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) Suppose such
a
exists.
Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K)
such that
\int|z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<r^{2}
.
Then

\int\log|\sigma(f)(z)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)\leq\int a(|z|^{2}-r^{2})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<ar^{2}-ar^{2}=0,

so
f
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Let
D_{0}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|z|<r\}
and
D_{\infty}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|z|>r\}
. For
c\in\{0,\infty\}
let

A_{c}=\big\{a\in\mathbb{R}\,\big|\,(\forall\,\rho\in\X(K))\,(\forall\,z\in D_{c})\ |\rho(f)(z)|\leq\exp(a(|z|^{2}-r^{2}))\big\}.

Firstly, we show that
A_{0}
is non-empty.
Let
\rho\in\X(K)
and
z_{0}\in D_{0}
, and let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K)
be the measure with weight
1
at
(\rho,z_{0})
.
Then
\int|x|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,x)=|z_{0}|^{2}<r^{2}
, so by exponential boundedness

|\rho(f)(z_{0})|=\exp\Big(\int\log|\sigma(f)(x)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,x)\Big)<1.

It follows that
0\in A_{0}
, and even
(-\infty,0]\subseteq A_{0}
. This argument also shows that
\rho(f)
is bounded by
1
on the boundary of
D_{0}
, the circle of radius
r
.

Secondly, we show that
A_{\infty}
is non-empty.
Since
\exp(|z|^{2}-r^{2})
grows faster than any polynomial, there exists some
b>r
such that
|\rho(f)(z)|\leq\exp(|z|^{2}-r^{2})
for all
|z|\geq b
.
Write
B=\{x\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|x|\leq b\}
.
Let
\rho\in\X(K)
and
z\in B\cap D_{\infty}
, and write
g=\rho(f)
and
\theta=z/|z|
.
As remarked at the end of the previous paragraph we have
|g(r\theta)|\leq 1
, so that

\displaystyle\log|g(z)|

\displaystyle\leq\log(1+|g(z)-g(r\theta)|)\leq|g(z)-g(r\theta)|=\frac{|z|^{2}-r^{2}}{|z|+r}\cdot\bigg|\frac{g(z)-g(r\theta)}{z-r\theta}\bigg|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\leq}}(|z|^{2}-r^{2})\cdot\frac{\sup_{x\in B}|g^{\prime}(x)|}{2r}\leq a(|z|^{2}-r^{2}),

where
*
follows from Lemma
17.5
and
a
is the maximum of
1
and all
(2r)^{-1}\sup_{x\in B}|\rho(f)^{\prime}(x)|
for
\rho\in\X(K)
. Thus
a\in A_{\infty}
.

Thirdly, we show that
A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}
is non-empty.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}
is empty.
Clearly
A_{0}
and
A_{\infty}
are closed.
Hence there exist reals that are neither in
A_{0}
nor
A_{\infty}
, and let
a
be such a real number.
It follows that
a>0
.
In turn, there exist
z_{0}\in D_{0}
and
z_{\infty}\in D_{\infty}
with
\rho_{0},\rho_{\infty}\in\X(K)
such that
|\rho_{0}(f)(z_{0})|>\exp(a(|z_{0}|^{2}-r^{2}))
and
|\rho_{\infty}(f)(z_{\infty})|>\exp(a(|z_{\infty}|^{2}-r^{2}))
.
Choose
t\in(0,1)
such that
(1-t)|z_{0}|^{2}+t|z_{\infty}|^{2}<r^{2}
and let
\mu
be the measure that assigns weight
1-t
to
(\rho_{0},z_{0})
and weight
t
to
(\rho_{\infty},z_{\infty})
. Then
\int|z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)<r^{2}
and thus

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle>\int\log|\sigma(f)(z)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\sigma,z)=(1-t)\log|\rho_{0}(f)(z_{0})|+t\log|\rho_{\infty}(f)(z_{\infty})|.

Taking the limit of
t
up to
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(1-s)|z_{0}|^{2}+s|z_{\infty}|^{2}=r^{2}
we get

0\geq(1-s)\log|\rho_{0}(f)(z_{0})|+s\log|\rho_{\infty}(f)(z_{\infty})|>a((1-s)|z_{0}|^{2}+s|z_{\infty}|^{2}-r^{2})=0,

a contradiction. Hence
A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}\neq\emptyset
, as was to be shown.

Note that
D_{0}\cup D_{\infty}
is dense in
\mathbb{C}
, so any positive
a\in A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}
gives the inequality we set out to prove.
Suppose
a\in A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}
is such that
a\leq 0
.
Thus
|\rho(f)(z)|\leq\exp(a(|z|^{2}-r^{2}))\leq 1
for all
z\in D_{\infty}
and
\rho\in\X(K)
, so
\rho(f)
is a constant function.
However, as
|\rho(f)(z)|<1
for
z\in D_{0}
as shown before, this constant is strictly less than
1
.
Let
c\in(0,1)
be a constant that bounds
\rho(f)
for all
\rho\in\X(K)
. Then
-r^{-2}\log c\in A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}
is positive.
Hence
A_{0}\cap A_{\infty}
always contains a positive element.
∎

18.
Volume computation

The next step is to compute the volume of a symmetric convex set of exponentially bounded polynomials.
As in Lemma
16.5
it suffices for the sake of volume computation to consider the case where the radius is
1
and the base field is
\mathbb{R}
.
In view of Theorem
17.6
, we consider the unit-ball of the following norm.

Definition 18.1
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. We equip
\mathbb{F}[Y]
with the norm

\|f\|_{\textup{e}}=\max_{z\in\mathbb{C}}\frac{|f(z)|}{\exp(|z|^{2})}.

Lemma 18.2
.

Consider the map
\phi:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by

\phi(n)=\begin{cases}\big(\frac{n}{2}\big)!&\textup{if $n$ is even}\\
\big(\frac{n-1}{2}\big)!\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{2}}&\textup{if $n$ is odd}\end{cases}.

Then we have

\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\phi(k)\Big)=\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n-(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n)

and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\frac{x^{2m+1}}{\phi(2m+1)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{x^{2m}}{\phi(2m)}+\frac{x^{2m+2}}{\phi(2m+1)}\bigg).

Proof.

Writing out the product we have

\prod_{k=0}^{n}\phi(k)=\Bigg(\prod_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\phi(2m)\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{m=0}^{\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor}\phi(2m+1)\Bigg)=\Bigg(\prod_{m=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}m!\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{m=0}^{\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor}m!\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{m=0}^{\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor}\sqrt{m+1}\Bigg).

We then apply Proposition
15.3
to compute

\displaystyle\smash{\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\phi(k)\Big)}\vphantom{\scalebox{2}{1}}

\displaystyle=\lfloor\tfrac{n}{2}\rfloor^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\lfloor\tfrac{n}{2}\rfloor-\tfrac{3}{4}\big)+\lfloor\tfrac{n-1}{2}\rfloor^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\lfloor\tfrac{n-1}{2}\rfloor-\tfrac{3}{4}\big)+\lfloor\tfrac{n-1}{2}\rfloor\big(\log\lfloor\tfrac{n-1}{2}\rfloor-1\big)+O(n\log n)

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{n}{2}\big)^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\tfrac{n}{2}-\tfrac{3}{4}\big)+\big(\tfrac{n}{2}\big)^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\tfrac{n}{2}-\tfrac{3}{4}\big)+0+O(n\log n)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n-(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n),

proving the first part. For the second, let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then

\frac{1}{\phi(2m)}+\frac{x^{2}}{\phi(2m+2)}=\frac{1}{m!}\Big(\Big(1-\frac{x}{\sqrt{m+1}}\Big)^{2}+\frac{2x}{\sqrt{m+1}}\Big)\geq\frac{1}{m!}\frac{2x}{\sqrt{m+1}}=2\cdot\frac{x}{\phi(2m+1)},

from which the second part follows.
∎

Recall the notation
R[X]_{n}
from Definition
12.6
, the subset of
R[X]
of polynomials of degree strictly less than
n
.

Proposition 18.3
.

Write
S=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[Y]\,|\,\|f\|_{\textup{e}}\leq 1\}
.
Then for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\log\vol(S\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n})\geq-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n+(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n).

Proof.

Consider
\phi
as in Lemma
18.2
and define

T=\Big\{\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}f_{i}Y^{i}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]\,\Big|\,(\forall i)\ |f_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2\phi(i)}\Big\}.

Then for all
f\in T
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have using Lemma
18.2
that

\displaystyle|f(z)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}|f_{i}|\cdot|z|^{i}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{|z|^{i}}{2\phi(i)}=\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|z|^{2k}}{k!}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|z|^{2k+1}}{\phi(2k+1)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|z|^{2k}}{k!}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{|z|^{2k}}{k!}+\frac{|z|^{2k+2}}{(k+1)!}\bigg)\Bigg]\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|z|^{2k}}{k!}=\exp|z^{2}|,

so
f\in S
and
T\subseteq S
. Then by Lemma
18.2
we have

\log\vol(T\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n})=-n\log 2-\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi(k)\Big)=-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n+(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n),

from which the proposition follows.
∎

Proposition
18.3
is sufficient for our purposes.
It may interest a reader attempting to improve our results that the lower bound of Proposition
18.3
is actually an equality, which we will show in the remainder of this section.

Theorem 18.4

(Brunn–Minkowski inequality, Theorem 2.1 in
[
6
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and let
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be bounded non-empty measurable sets.
Then for all
t\in[0,1]
such that

(1-t)A+tB=\{(1-t)a+tb\,|\,a\in A,\,b\in B\}

is measurable we have the inequality

\textup{vol}((1-t)A+tB)^{1/n}\geq(1-t)\textup{vol}(A)^{1/n}+t\textup{vol}(B)^{1/n}.\qed

We will only apply this theorem to compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which are indeed measurable and bounded.
Moreover, for
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
compact and
t\in(0,1)
also the set
(1-t)A+tB
is compact, hence measurable.

Corollary 18.5
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a subspace and let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a symmetric convex body.
Then the map that sends
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\vol_{V}(V\cap(S-x))
takes a maximum at
0
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and write
m=\dim V
and
H_{x}=V\cap(S-x)
.
If
m=0
the corollary holds trivially, so suppose
m>0
.
Note that
H_{-x}=-H_{x}
since
S
and
V
are symmetric.
Because
S
and
V
are convex we have
\tfrac{1}{2}H_{x}+\tfrac{1}{2}H_{-x}\subseteq H_{0}
.
Hence by Theorem
18.4
we have

\textup{vol}(H_{0})^{1/m}\geq\textup{vol}(\tfrac{1}{2}H_{x}+\tfrac{1}{2}H_{-x})^{1/m}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\textup{vol}(H_{x})^{1/m}+\tfrac{1}{2}\textup{vol}(H_{-x})^{1/m}=\textup{vol}(H_{x})^{1/m},

from which the corollary follows.
∎

Recall the definition of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox to6.75pt{\vbox to6.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3742pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.48 0 C 4.48 2.48 2.48 4.48 0 4.48 C -2.48 4.48 -4.48 2.48 -4.48 0 C -4.48 -2.48 -2.48 -4.48 0 -4.48 C 2.48 -4.48 4.48 -2.48 4.48 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
