\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox to6.75pt{\vbox to6.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3742pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.48 0 C 4.48 2.48 2.48 4.48 0 4.48 C -2.48 4.48 -4.48 2.48 -4.48 0 C -4.48 -2.48 -2.48 -4.48 0 -4.48 C 2.48 -4.48 4.48 -2.48 4.48 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.48 L 0 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.07 -1.9 L 4.07 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}V\to U
.
If
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a symmetric convex body, then
\vol_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(S)\leq\vol_{U}(\pi S)\cdot\vol_{V}(S\cap V)
.

Proof.

By Corollary
18.5
we have

\vol(S)=\int_{\pi S}\vol_{V}(V\cap(S-x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\pi S}\vol_{V}(V\cap S)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\vol_{U}(\pi S)\cdot\vol_{V}(V\cap S).

∎

Theorem 18.7
.

Write
S=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[Y]\,|\,\|f\|_{\textup{e}}\leq 1\}
.
Then for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\log\vol(S\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n})=-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n+(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n).

Proof.

We already proved a lower bound in Proposition
18.3
, so it remains to prove an upper bound.
We will inductively show that

\vol(S\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n})\leq 2^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\Big(\frac{2\textup{e}}{k}\Big)^{k/2}.

It then follows from Proposition
15.3
that

\log\vol(S\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n})\leq n\log 2+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k\big(\log(2\textup{e})-\log k)=-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n+(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}+O(n\log n).

For
n=0
and
n=1
the inequality certainly holds.
Now suppose the inequality holds for
n\geq 1
.
Write
\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n+1}=(\mathbb{R}Y^{n})\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox to6.75pt{\vbox to6.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3742pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.48 0 C 4.48 2.48 2.48 4.48 0 4.48 C -2.48 4.48 -4.48 2.48 -4.48 0 C -4.48 -2.48 -2.48 -4.48 0 -4.48 C 2.48 -4.48 4.48 -2.48 4.48 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.48 L 0 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.07 -1.9 L 4.07 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\textstyle\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox to6.75pt{\vbox to6.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3742pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.48 0 C 4.48 2.48 2.48 4.48 0 4.48 C -2.48 4.48 -4.48 2.48 -4.48 0 C -4.48 -2.48 -2.48 -4.48 0 -4.48 C 2.48 -4.48 4.48 -2.48 4.48 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.48 L 0 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.07 -1.9 L 4.07 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox to6.75pt{\vbox to6.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3742pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.48 0 C 4.48 2.48 2.48 4.48 0 4.48 C -2.48 4.48 -4.48 2.48 -4.48 0 C -4.48 -2.48 -2.48 -4.48 0 -4.48 C 2.48 -4.48 4.48 -2.48 4.48 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.48 L 0 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.07 -1.9 L 4.07 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\raisebox{2.4pt}{\hbox to6.75pt{\vbox to6.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3742pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 4.48 0 C 4.48 2.48 2.48 4.48 0 4.48 C -2.48 4.48 -4.48 2.48 -4.48 0 C -4.48 -2.48 -2.48 -4.48 0 -4.48 C 2.48 -4.48 4.48 -2.48 4.48 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.48 L 0 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.07 -1.9 L 4.07 -1.9}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n}
and let
\pi:\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}Y^{n}
be the projection map.
By Corollary
18.6
it suffices to show for
n>0
that
\vol(\pi(S\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n+1}))\leq 2(\frac{n}{2\textup{e}})^{-n/2}
.
We do this by proving

\pi(S\cap\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{(i)}}}{{\subseteq}}S\cap(\mathbb{R}Y^{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{(ii)}}}{{\subseteq}}[-1,+1]\Big(\frac{2\textup{e}}{n}\Big)^{n/2}Y^{n}.

(i) Suppose
f\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n+1}
and
\|f\|_{\textup{e}}<\|\pi(f)\|_{\textup{e}}
.
Since
\pi(f)
is a monomial, the function
z\mapsto|\pi(f)(z)|\exp(-|z|^{2})
takes its maximum on a circle of radius say
r
.
Then for all
z
on this circle we have
|f(z)|\leq\|f\|_{\textup{e}}\exp(r^{2})<\|\pi(f)\|_{\textup{e}}\exp(r^{2})=|\pi(f)(z)|
.
Hence by Rouché’s theorem, the polynomial
f-\pi(f)
has as many roots as
\pi(f)
in the disk
\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|z|\leq r\}
, counting multiplicities.
However, since
f-\pi(f)
has degree at most
n-1
and
\pi(f)
has
n
such roots, this is a contradiction.
Hence
\|\pi(f)\|_{\textup{e}}\leq\|f\|_{\textup{e}}
, from which (i) follows.

(ii) Consider the map
g:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by
x\mapsto x^{n}\exp(-x^{2})
.
Then

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}=x^{n-1}(n-2x^{2})\exp(-x^{2})=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ x=0\ \lor\ x=\sqrt{n/2}.

Hence
g
takes a maximum at
(n/2)^{1/2}
.
We conclude that
\|Y^{n}\|_{\textup{e}}=g((n/2)^{1/2})=(\tfrac{n}{2\textup{e}})^{n/2}
.
Thus
\max\{c\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,cY^{n}\in S\}=(\tfrac{2\textup{e}}{n})^{n/2}
, as was to be shown.

The theorem now follows by induction.
∎

19.
Proof of the main theorem

We are now ready to give a proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Proposition 19.1
.

Let
R
be an order of a number field
K
, let
\alpha\in K
and
0<r^{2}<\frac{1}{2}\exp(\frac{1}{2})
.
Then there exists some non-zero
f\in R[X]
such that
f(X-\alpha)
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
d=[K:\mathbb{Q}]
.
Write
Y=X-\alpha
and consider the real vector space
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}
, which we equip with an inner product as in Definition
16.2
with respect to the variable
Y
.
By Theorem
12.5
and Lemma
16.4
the lattice
R[X]_{n}
in
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}
is full rank and has determinant
\det(R[Y]_{n})=\left|\det(R)\right|^{n}=|\Delta(R)|^{n/2}
.
For
b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
consider

\displaystyle S_{n}

\displaystyle=\{f\in K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}\,|\,(\forall\,\sigma\in\X(K))\,(\forall\,z\in\mathbb{C})\ |\sigma(f)(z)|\leq\exp(bn(|z|^{2}-r^{2}))\}

\displaystyle=\{f\in K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}\,|\,(\forall\,\sigma\in\X(K))\ \|\exp(bnr^{2})\cdot\sigma(f)((bn)^{-1/2}\cdot Y)\|_{\textup{e}}\leq 1\}.

We have a natural orthogonal decomposition
K_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{u}\times\mathbb{C}^{v}
for some
u,v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
which in turn gives an orthogonal decomposition
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]_{n}=(\mathbb{R}[Y]_{n})^{u}\times(\mathbb{C}[Y]_{n})^{v}
.
Note that
S_{n}
is simply a product over
\sigma\in\X(K)
of

S_{n}(\sigma)=\{f\in\mathbb{F}[Y]_{n}\,|\,\|\exp(bnr^{2})\cdot\sigma(f)((bn)^{-1/2}\cdot Y)\|_{\textup{e}}\leq 1\}

where
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
depending on whether
\sigma(K)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.
Then using Lemma
16.4
and Proposition
18.3
we compute

\displaystyle\log\vol(S_{n})

\displaystyle\geq d\Big(\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log n+(\tfrac{3}{8}+\tfrac{1}{4}\log 2)n^{2}\big)+\big(\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2}\log(bn)-bn^{2}r^{2}\big)\Big)+O(n\log n)

\displaystyle=dn^{2}\cdot\epsilon(b)+O(n\log n),

with
\epsilon(b)=\tfrac{1}{4}\log(2b)+\tfrac{3}{8}-r^{2}b
. Choosing
b=(2r)^{-2}
we get
\epsilon(b)=\tfrac{1}{4}(\tfrac{1}{2}-\log(2r^{2}))>0
.
Hence

\log\Big(\frac{\vol(S_{n})}{2^{dn}\cdot|\Delta(R)|^{n/2}}\Big)\geq dn^{2}\cdot\epsilon(b)+O(n\log n)\to\infty\quad(\text{as }n\to\infty).

Thus by Minkowski’s theorem there exists for
n
sufficiently large some non-zero
g\in S_{n}\cap R[X]
.
Because
g\in S_{n}
, this polynomial is exponentially bounded at radius
r
by Theorem
17.6
.
∎

Theorem
1.4
.
If
\gamma\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
q(\gamma)<2\exp(\tfrac{1}{2})\approx 3.297
, then
\gamma
has only finitely many decompositions.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=\gamma/2
, let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)
and let
R
be some order in
K
.
Choose
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
q(\alpha)<r^{2}<\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(\tfrac{1}{2})
.
Then by Proposition
19.1
there exists some non-zero polynomial
f\in R[X]
which as polynomial in
Y=X-\alpha
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
. Hence by Proposition
17.3
all
(\beta,\gamma-\beta)\in\text{dec}(\gamma)
satisfy
f(\beta)=0
.
As
f
has only finitely many roots, the theorem follows.
∎

20.
Remarks on the proof of the main theorem

In this section we briefly discuss the proof of Theorem
1.4
and make some practical remarks for explicit computation.

The proof of Theorem
1.4
proceeds in the following steps:

(1)

We determine a sufficient condition for a polynomial to have all decompositions of
\alpha
as roots.

(2)

We translate this condition into an analytic one.

(3)

We determine the volume of a symmetric convex set of polynomials satisfying this condition.

(4)

We apply Minkowski’s convex body theorem to find integral polynomials in this set.

Theorem
17.6
suggests that step (2) can hardly be improved upon.
By Theorem
18.7
we correctly computed the volume of our symmetric convex set in step (3).
However, in order to make it convex we fixed the constant
a
that comes out of Theorem
17.6
.
It is easy to verify that we indeed made an optimal choice of
a
in Proposition
19.1
, although that does not guarantee we chose the best convex subset.
If the weakest link in the proof is step (4), we likely require a completely different approach.
It should be noted however that Minkowski’s convex body theorem is powerful enough to prove the classical Theorem
16.6
.

A piece of information we did not exploit in our proof of the main theorem is the following symmetry.

Proposition 20.1
.

For all
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
we have an involution
x\mapsto\alpha-x
on
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
which induces action on
\textup{dec}(\alpha)
given by
(\beta,\gamma)\mapsto(\gamma,\beta)
. ∎

Corollary 20.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and let
\mathbb{Z}[\alpha]\subseteq R
be an order of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.
For all
f\in R[X]
such that
f(\beta)=0
for all
(\beta,\gamma)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
, also
g=f(\alpha-X)\in R[X]
satisfies
g(\beta)=0
for all
(\beta,\gamma)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
. ∎

Corollary
20.2
turns the involution on
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
into an
R
-algebra automorphism on
R[X]
.
We can incorporate this automorphism in our proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Proposition 20.3
.

Let
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
\mathbb{Z}[\alpha]\subseteq R
be an order of
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
For all
f\in R[X]
such that
f
as a polynomial in
Y=X-\alpha/2
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
, so is
f(X)\cdot f(\alpha-X)\in K[Y^{2}]
.

Proof.

Note that the involution
X\mapsto\alpha-X
is with respect to
Y
given by
Y\mapsto-Y
.
Hence if
f
as a polynomial in
Y
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
, then so is
f(\alpha-X)
.
As noted in Example
17.4
, the set of polynomials exponentially bounded at
r
is closed under multiplication.
Hence
g=f(X)\cdot f(\alpha-X)
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
.
Now
g
is invariant under
Y\mapsto-Y
, meaning all coefficients at odd degree monomials in
Y
are zero, i.e.
g\in K[Y^{2}]
.
∎

An interesting question to ask is how dissimilar
f
and
f(\alpha-X)
can be for exponentially bounded
f
.
Certainly both should have
\beta
as root for all
(\beta,\gamma)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
by Corollary
20.2
.
In the context of finding ‘small’
f
algorithmically it seems that often
f
and
f(\alpha-X)
are the same (up to sign).

As a consequence of Proposition
20.3
, in the proof of Theorem
1.4
we may look at the lattice
R[X(\alpha-X)]
in
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y^{2}]
instead of
R[X]
in
K_{\mathbb{R}}[Y]
.
The effect is two-fold.
Firstly, it simplifies the volume computation of Proposition
18.3
, as we no longer require the ad-hoc function
\phi
from Lemma
18.2
.
Secondly, any integral polynomial in our symmetric body can be found in a lower dimensional lattice in Proposition
19.1
.
This follows from the suggested changes to Proposition
18.3
, but can heuristically be seen as follows.
If
f
is a solution in the original lattice
R[X]
, then
f(X)\cdot f(\alpha-X)
exists in our new lattice
R[X(\alpha-X)]
at the same dimension.
However, as discussed before,
f
might already be an element of
R[X(\alpha-X)]
anyway, so we could have already found
f
at half the dimension.
Neither of these changes have an effect on the quality of our theoretical results.
However, when we want to compute decompositions in practice, the latter is very useful.

21.
Computing decompositions of algebraic integers of small square-norm

From the proof of Theorem
1.4
one easily derives the following result.

Theorem 21.1
.

There exists an algorithm that takes as input an element
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, and decides whether
q(\alpha)<2\sqrt{\textup{e}}
and if so, computes all decompositions of
\alpha
.

Proof.

Clearly
q(\alpha)\neq 2\sqrt{\textup{e}}
as the latter is not algebraic. However, both are computable, and after finitely many steps of approximation we can decide whether
q(\alpha)<2\sqrt{\textup{e}}
.
If so, we can even compute a rational
r
such that
q(\alpha)<r^{2}<2\sqrt{\textup{e}}
.

We have an explicit formula for a lower bound on the volume of the set
S
as defined in the proof of Proposition
18.3
.
Thus we can compute a sufficiently large
n
such that Minkowski’s theorem, as in the proof of Proposition
19.1
, guarantees the existence of a non-zero lattice point in
S
. We may then simply enumerate all lattice points to eventually find a polynomial
f
as in Proposition
19.1
. We determine using Theorem
14.2
the monic irreducible factors of
f
and decide using Corollary
14.7
for each whether it gives rise to a decomposition of
\alpha
.
∎

22.
Working out an example

We will now work out an example proving an algebraic integer
\alpha
is indecomposable, proceeding along the lines of our lattice algorithm.
Of course, this result will be trivial when
q(\alpha)\leq 2
as we have seen in Proposition
8.1
, so we will choose
\alpha
such that
q(\alpha)>2
.
On the other hand, the algorithms from Section
21
terminate faster the smaller
q(\alpha)
is, so for this example we will consider
\alpha=\sqrt[3]{3}
with
q(\alpha)=3^{2/3}\approx 2.080
.

Setup.
Let
\alpha=\sqrt[3]{3}
and let
r^{2}=6/11
, so that
q(\alpha/2)<r^{2}<\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(\tfrac{1}{2})
. Write
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and
R=\mathbb{Z}[\alpha]
and consider the ring
R[X]
.
Writing
Y=X-\alpha/2
, we are looking for a polynomial
f\in R[X]
such that
f
as polynomial in
Y
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
.
However, writing
Z=X(\alpha-X)
we may instead look for such a polynomial in
R[Z]
, as follows from Proposition
20.3
.

Finding a polynomial.

It is quite involved to systematically find short vectors in a lattice.
Instead we will employ a more ad-hoc approach, more along the lines of Theorem
13.7
.
We guess that our polynomial
f
will be monic in
Z
of some degree
n
.
We start with
Z^{n}
and then greedily subtract
\mathbb{Z}[\alpha]
-multiples of lower degree powers of
Z
such that the resulting polynomial in
Y
becomes ‘small’, i.e. has small coefficients under every embedding
K\to\mathbb{C}
with lower degree terms weighing more heavily.
Efficitively, we are applying a rounding function in the sense of Lemma
13.4
.
Note that
Z=-Y^{2}+\alpha^{2}/4
.
Similarly as in the proof of Theorem
13.7
, taking
n=4
the
Y^{6}
term becomes integral, which is useful.
Thus we will try
n=4
. We compute:

Z^4

=

Y^8

-
α
^2 Y^6

+

9

8

α
Y^4

-

9

16

Y^2

+

9

256

α
^2

α
^2 Z^3

=

-
α
^2 Y^6

+

9

4

α
Y^4

-

27

16

Y^2

+

9

64

α
^2

Z^4

-
α
^2 Z^3

=

Y^8

-

9

8

α
Y^4

+

9

8

Y^2

-

27

256

α
^2

-
α
Z^2 =

-
α
Y^4

+

3

2

Y^2

-

3

16

α
^2

Z^4

-
α
^2 Z^3

+
α
Z^2 =

Y^8

-

1

8

α
Y^4

-

3

8

Y^2

+

21

256

α
^2

The remaining coefficients with respect to
Y
look pretty small in every embedding
K\to\mathbb{C}
, so we guess

f(Y)=Y^{8}-\tfrac{1}{8}\alpha Y^{4}-\tfrac{3}{8}Y^{2}+\tfrac{21}{256}\alpha^{2}=Z^{4}-\alpha^{2}Z^{3}+\alpha Z^{2}\in R[Z].

is going to be exponentially bounded at radius
r
as polynomial in
Y
.

Proving exponentially boundedness.
If we take
b:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by

b(w)=w^{4}+\tfrac{1}{8}\cdot 3^{1/3}\cdot w^{2}+\tfrac{3}{8}\cdot w+\tfrac{21}{256}\cdot 3^{2/3},

then for all
\sigma\in\X(K)
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have
|\sigma(f)(z)|\leq b(|z|^{2})
.
To prove that
f
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
it suffices to find
a\in\mathbb{R}
such that
b(w)\leq\exp(a(w-r^{2}))
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Because
b(0)=\frac{21}{256}3^{2/3}
, we must have
a\leq-\log(b(0))/r^{2}\approx 3.4
.
We will try
a=3
for simplicity.
Consider the function
B(w)=b(w)\cdot\exp(-a(w-r^{2}))
, for which we want to show
B(w)\leq 1
for all
w
.
Then

0=B^{\prime}(w)=\exp(-a(w-r^{2}))(b^{\prime}(w)-ab(w))

if and only if
ab(w)-b^{\prime}(w)=0
.
Since the latter is simply a polynomial equation we will find using standard techniques that it has no positive real roots.
We compute:

\displaystyle ab(w)-b^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=3w^{4}-4w^{3}+\tfrac{3}{8}3^{1/3}w^{2}+(\tfrac{9}{8}-\tfrac{1}{4}3^{1/3})w+(\tfrac{63}{256}3^{2/3}-\tfrac{3}{8})

\displaystyle>3w^{4}-4w^{3}+\tfrac{3}{5}w^{2}+\tfrac{3}{4}w+\tfrac{1}{4}.

For
1\leq w
we get
ab(w)-b^{\prime}(w)>3w^{4}-4w^{3}+\tfrac{3}{2}=w^{2}(3^{1/2}w-2\cdot 3^{-1/2})^{2}+(\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{4}{3}w^{2})\geq 0
and for
0<w\leq 1
we get

ab(w)-b^{\prime}(w)>3w^{4}-4w^{3}+\tfrac{3}{2}w^{2}=3w^{2}(w^{2}-\tfrac{4}{3}w+\tfrac{1}{2})\geq 3w^{2}(w-2^{-1/2})^{2}\geq 0
.
Hence
B
has no local maxima besides possibly at
0
, and because
B(w)\to 0
as
w\to\infty
we conclude that
B
takes a maximum at
0
.
Therefore
b
is bounded by
w\mapsto\exp(a(w-r^{2}))
and thus
f
is exponentially bounded at radius
r
.

Finding decompositions.

Writing
f
as a polynomial in
X
we get

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=X^{8}-4\alpha X^{7}+7\alpha^{2}X^{6}-21X^{5}+13\alpha X^{4}-5\alpha^{2}X^{3}+3X^{2}

\displaystyle=X^{2}\cdot(\alpha-X)^{2}\cdot(X^{4}-2\alpha X^{3}+2\alpha^{2}X^{2}-3X+\alpha).

By Proposition
17.3
all decompositions of
\alpha
can be found among the roots of
f
.
The factors
X
and
\alpha-X
correspond to the trivial decompositions
(0,\alpha)
and
(\alpha,0)
of
\alpha
.
The polynomial
h=X^{4}-2\alpha X^{3}+2\alpha^{2}X^{2}-3X+\alpha
is irreducible as it is Eisenstein at the prime
(\alpha)
.
Let
\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
be a root of
h
.
By Lemma
7.7
we have
q(\beta)\geq N(\beta)^{2}=N(h(0))^{2}=3^{2/3}=q(\alpha)
.
We can only have
q(\beta)+q(\alpha-\beta)\leq q(\alpha)
if
q(\alpha-\beta)=0
, i.e.
\alpha=\beta
, which is impossible.
Hence
\alpha
is indecomposable by Lemma
4.3
.

23.
Enumeration of indecomposable algebraic integers of degree 3

In this section we discuss our attempt to compute the indecomposable algebraic integers of degree 3 and derive Theorem
1.5
.
These results are obtained by a computer program written in Sage.

We will write
f_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
for the minimal polynomial of
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We save ourselves some work by only considering
\alpha
up to ‘trivial isometries’ of
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, namely those of
\mu_{\infty}\rtimes\textup{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
as in Lemma
7.13
.
Using Proposition
15.1
we can compute a set of
5525
polynomials among which we can find all minimal polynomials of the indecomposable algebraic integers of degree 3.
Among those
5525
polynomials
f
only
700
are in fact irreducible with
q(\alpha)\leq 4
for all roots
\alpha
of
f
.
We already eliminated the Galois action by considering minimal polynomials instead of elements, and by choosing only one element of each
\mu_{\infty}
-orbit
\{f,-f(-x)\}
we eliminate the action of
\mu_{\infty}
, and end up with ‘only’
350
polynomials to check.
Of those
350
, there are
27
polynomials
f_{\alpha}
such that
q(\alpha)<2
, so that
\alpha
is indecomposable by Proposition
8.1
.

Small degree decompositions.

For
95
polynomials
f
, the roots
\alpha
of
f
have a non-trivial decomposition in the ring of integers of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.
For
116
of the remaining polynomials
f_{\alpha}
we can find a non-trivial decomposition
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)
of
\alpha
with
\beta
in the ring of integers of a degree 2 extension of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.
Of those
116
there are
84
for which the minimal polynomial
g_{\beta}
of
\beta
over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
is of the form
x^{2}-\alpha x\pm 1
, a polynomial we encountered in the proof of Lemma
11.2
.
The remaining
32
polynomials and corresponding decompositions can be found in Table
1
.
We are now left with
112
polynomials to check.

Table 1
.

A table of minimal polynomials
f_{\alpha}
together with a minimal polynomial
g_{\beta}
of degree 2 over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
such that
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)
is a non-trivial decomposition of
\alpha
.

Large degree decompositions.

To find decompositions in higher degree extensions we implemented a lattice algorithm.
Since we are interested in finding only one decomposition instead of all of them, and since verifying whether something is a decomposition is computationally easy, we can get away with a lot of heuristics.
For
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)\in\textup{dec}(\alpha)
we have, on average of squares over all embeddings of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,\beta)
in
\mathbb{C}
, that
|\beta-\alpha/2|\leq\sqrt{q(\alpha/2)}=r
by Lemma
4.3
. Hence if we write
g_{\beta}=\sum_{i}c_{i}(x-\alpha/2)^{i}
we have that
\sum_{i}|c_{i}|r^{i}
should be small.
It is useful for our lattice algorithm to instead consider the 2-norm
(\sum_{i}r^{i}\sum_{\sigma}|\sigma(c_{i})|^{2})^{1/2}
and hope this does not affect the quality of our results for the worse.
We enumerate small polynomials
\epsilon\in\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)[x]
of degree less than
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
(x-\alpha/2)^{d}-x^{d}+\epsilon
is in the lattice of integral polynomials, and thus
(x-\alpha/2)^{d}+\epsilon
is monic, integral and small.
We then verify for each of those whether they induce a decomposition of
\alpha
.
The
41
polynomials
f_{\alpha}
for which this method has found a non-trivial decomposition
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)
of
\alpha
with
g_{\beta}
of degree greater than
2
are listed in Table
2
together with the polynomial
g_{\beta}
found.
This leaves
71
polynomials to check and gives an upper bound of
6\cdot 98=588
on the number of indecomposable algebraic integers of degree 3.

Table 2
.

A table of minimal polynomials
f_{\alpha}
together with a minimal polynomial
g_{\beta}
of degree greater than 2 over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
such that
(\beta,\alpha-\beta)
is a non-trivial decomposition of
\alpha
.

Indecomposables.

On the other hand, we want to prove that certain
\alpha
are indecomposable.
To this end, we implemented a lattice algorithm similar to that of Theorem
21.1
.
To hopefully speed up the algorithm we also apply the dimension reducing symmetry trick discussed in Section
20
.
Writing
R
for the ring of integers of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
and
z=x(\alpha-x)
, we enumerate short
g\in R[z]
for which we verify whether
g
as polynomial in
y=x-\alpha/2
is exponentially bounded.
The
32
polynomials
f_{\alpha}
for which we found such a
g
proving indecomposability of
\alpha
are listed in Table
3
.
We present
g
in factored form for compactness.
This leaves
39
polynomials undetermined and gives a lower bound of
6\cdot 59=354
on the number of indecomposable algebraic integers of degree
3
.

g

Table 3
.

A table of minimal polynomials
f_{\alpha}
together with a polynomial
g
over
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
such that
g(x+\alpha/2)
is exponentially bounded at radius
\sqrt{q(\alpha/2)}
, proving
\alpha
is indecomposable.

Theorem
1.5
.
There are exactly
2
indecomposable algebraic integers of degree
1
, there are exactly
14
of degree
2
, and there are at least
354
and at most
588
of degree
3
.

Proof.

The degree
1
case is obvious:
1
and
-1
are the only indecomposable integers. The degree
2
case is Theorem
11.3
.
The bounds for degree
3
are the result of the computation in this section.
∎
